## #1052148 - 2026-06-02 13:24:57 UTC - @punk6529 - WINNER

Drop ID: `d2613993-2714-4618-b2db-8175f395cea6`

**Proposal 1: Autoglyphs**

The 6529 Network Museum considers Autoglyphs to be within scope for the Museum’s collection.

Donations of Autoglyphs may be accepted without further collection-specific review, subject only to the Museum’s ordinary procedures for provenance, title, custody, wallet security, legal compliance, and donor acceptance.


**Rationale**

Autoglyphs are a canonical work of on-chain generative art. They represent one of the clearest and narrowest cases for inclusion: a historically important, finite, fully on-chain generative art project with broad recognition across the digital art field.


Approving Autoglyphs as a standing eligible category gives the Museum a safe initial boundary. It does not require the Museum to define the full outer limits of on-chain generative art, nor does it imply that other collections are excluded. It simply recognizes that Autoglyphs are sufficiently central to the field that they should not require repeated collection-level review each time a donation opportunity arises.


Acquisition programs involving Autoglyphs may also be pursued, provided they are approved, funded, and executed through the Museum’s ordinary governance, budgeting, and acquisition processes.

## #1052156 - 2026-06-02 13:27:00 UTC - @punk6529 - WINNER

Drop ID: `2e88273f-013c-4fdd-bea3-7de5451098e8`

**Proposal 2: Art Blocks**


The 6529 Network Museum considers Art Blocks collections to be within scope for the Museum’s collection.


Donations of works from Art Blocks collections may be accepted without further collection-specific review, subject only to the Museum’s ordinary procedures for provenance, title, custody, wallet security, legal compliance, and donor acceptance.


**Rationale**


Art Blocks is one of the defining platforms for on-chain generative art. Its model, in which artists publish algorithmic systems that generate unique works on-chain at the moment of minting, is directly aligned with the Museum’s interest in digital art, open networks, algorithmic culture, and blockchain-native creative practice.


Approving Art Blocks as a standing eligible category gives the Museum a practical and defensible starting point for collecting on-chain generative art. It creates a clear safe harbor for a major, field-defining platform without deciding in advance how every other generative art project should be treated.


This approval should not be read to exclude other on-chain generative art collections. Other collections may be reviewed and accepted through the Museum’s ordinary processes.


Acquisition programs involving Art Blocks works may also be pursued, provided they are approved, funded, and executed through the Museum’s ordinary governance, budgeting, and acquisition processes.

## #1052401 - 2026-06-02 14:04:58 UTC - @punk6529 - WINNER

Drop ID: `457056cf-3090-4332-ba37-196b10f5f5d2`

**Proposal 3: Rare Pepes**


The 6529 Network Museum considers the original Rare Pepe collection to be within scope for the Museum’s collection.


Donations of original Rare Pepe cards may be accepted without further collection-specific review, subject only to the Museum’s ordinary procedures for provenance, title, authenticity, custody, wallet security, legal compliance, donor acceptance, and any artwork-specific review required by Museum policy.


**Rationale**


Rare Pepes are a foundational early example of blockchain-native visual culture. They sit at the intersection of memes, digital collecting, internet-native authorship, community curation, scarce digital objects, and early NFT-like infrastructure. For a museum concerned with the history of open metaverse culture, blockchain-native art, and networked digital expression, Rare Pepes are not merely collectibles; they are primary historical artifacts.


Rare Pepes are also directly relevant to the 6529 ecosystem. The Meme Cards are modeled in various ways on Rare Pepes, among other precedents. The relationship is not one of simple replication, but Rare Pepes clearly helped establish several ideas that later became important to The Memes: the card format, serialized meme-native works, community recognition, collection-building around cultural symbols, and the idea that memes can function as durable cultural objects rather than disposable internet ephemera.


Approving the original Rare Pepe collection as a standing eligible category gives the Museum a narrow and defensible safe harbor. It recognizes the historical importance of Rare Pepes without requiring the Museum to approve every Pepe-related or meme-related project in advance.


This approval should not be read to imply that all Pepe derivatives, meme coins, later derivative projects, or meme-based NFT collections are automatically within scope. Those may be reviewed separately through the Museum’s ordinary processes.


Acquisition programs involving original Rare Pepe cards may also be pursued, provided they are approved, funded, and executed through the Museum’s ordinary governance, budgeting, and acquisition processes.

## #1052437 - 2026-06-02 14:13:36 UTC - @punk6529 - WINNER

Drop ID: `e7999acd-4e06-44fc-8f40-bd8d55dc91d1`

**Proposal 4: CryptoPunks**

The 6529 Network Museum considers the original CryptoPunks collection to be within scope for the Museum’s collection.


Donations of original CryptoPunks may be accepted without further collection-specific review, subject only to the Museum’s ordinary procedures for provenance, title, authenticity, custody, wallet security, legal compliance, donor acceptance, and any artwork-specific review required by Museum policy.


**Rationale**

CryptoPunks are a canonical work of blockchain-native art and digital collecting. They are among the clearest cases for inclusion in a museum concerned with NFTs, open metaverse culture, digital ownership, avatar identity, blockchain-native art, and the emergence of scarce digital objects.


CryptoPunks are important not only as individual images, but as a historical system: a fixed set of 10,000 characters, algorithmically generated, owned and transferred on Ethereum, and organized around traits, rarity, typology, identity, and market exchange. They helped establish much of the visual, technical, and cultural vocabulary that later NFT collections inherited.


Approving the original CryptoPunks collection as a standing eligible category gives the Museum a narrow and defensible safe harbor. It recognizes the project’s foundational status without extending automatic approval to all profile-picture collections, avatar projects, derivatives, or later NFT collectibles.


This approval should not be read to imply that all PFP collections, CryptoPunks derivatives, or avatar-based NFT projects are automatically within scope. Those may be reviewed separately through the Museum’s ordinary processes.


Acquisition programs involving original CryptoPunks may also be pursued, provided they are approved, funded, and executed through the Museum’s ordinary governance, budgeting, and acquisition processes.

## #1052604 - 2026-06-02 14:50:40 UTC - @punk6529 - WINNER

Drop ID: `d65befc2-65dc-4362-8ddd-75f867338669`

**Proposal: General NFT Collecting Scope**


The 6529 Network Museum considers historically, artistically, culturally, or technically significant NFTs and blockchain-native digital objects to be within scope for the Museum’s collection.


This scope includes, without limitation:


1. **PFPs and avatar projects**

   Collections that contributed meaningfully to digital identity, community formation, avatar culture, online self-representation, or the evolution of NFT-native collecting.


2. **Generative art**

   Algorithmic, procedural, on-chain, long-form, or otherwise system-based digital artworks, including both fully on-chain and partially on-chain works.


3. **1/1 digital art**

   Unique digital artworks issued as NFTs, including works by artists using NFTs as a native medium, distribution mechanism, provenance layer, or market structure.


4. **Editions**

   Limited, open, timed, or otherwise editioned NFT artworks, including works where the edition structure itself is part of the artistic, cultural, or distribution logic.


5. **NFT photography**

   Photographic works issued as NFTs, including both single-image works and editioned photographic projects, where the NFT meaningfully contributes to provenance, distribution, community, or artistic context.


6. **Meme-native and internet-culture works**

   NFTs and blockchain-native objects rooted in memes, remix culture, networked communities, viral images, internet-native symbols, or shared cultural references.


7. **Interactive, dynamic, and programmable works**

   NFTs that change over time, respond to data, include interactive elements, depend on smart contracts, or otherwise use blockchain infrastructure as part of the work.


8. **AI, computational, and software-based works**

   Works created with, about, or through artificial intelligence, code, software systems, autonomous processes, or computational media.


9. **Video, audio, literary, and mixed-media NFTs**

   NFT-native works using moving image, sound, music, poetry, text, performance documentation, or hybrid digital media.


10. **Virtual-world, gaming, and metaverse-native objects**

    Digital objects, wearables, land, environments, game assets, or artifacts that are historically or culturally significant to open metaverse development, provided they have sufficient curatorial relevance.


11. **Protocol-native and platform-native cultural artifacts**

    Blockchain-native objects that may not fit traditional art categories but are important to the history of NFTs, digital ownership, crypto culture, open networks, or the development of decentralized cultural systems.


The inclusion of a category within scope does not mean that every work or collection in that category must be accepted. Individual works, collections, and acquisition programs remain subject to the Museum’s ordinary procedures for curatorial review, provenance, title, authenticity, custody, wallet security, legal compliance, donor acceptance, budgeting, and governance.


The Museum may also establish specific safe-harbor categories or pre-approved collections from time to time. Where such a safe harbor has been approved, donations from that collection may be accepted without further collection-specific review, while still remaining subject to the Museum’s ordinary operational, legal, provenance, and custody checks.


Acquisition programs involving NFTs or blockchain-native digital objects may be pursued, provided they are approved, funded, and executed through the Museum’s ordinary governance, budgeting, and acquisition processes.


**Rationale**


The NFT field is broader than any single format. Some of its most important works are generative art projects; others are PFPs, 1/1s, editions, photographs, memes, dynamic works, virtual-world objects, or artifacts that are difficult to classify using older museum categories.


The 6529 Network Museum should therefore define its collecting scope broadly enough to recognize the full range of blockchain-native culture. NFTs are not merely a wrapper for digital images. In many cases, the token, contract, provenance, community, minting structure, edition logic, ownership history, and network context are part of the work’s meaning.


At the same time, a broad collecting scope should not become automatic approval for every NFT project. The better structure is to distinguish between category-level eligibility and collection-level approval. Broad categories can be in scope, while specific works and collections remain subject to review unless separately approved as safe harbors.


This approach gives the Museum flexibility, intellectual seriousness, and operational discipline. It allows the Museum to collect across the full history of NFT-native culture while preserving clear standards for acceptance, acquisition, and long-term stewardship.

## #1052714 - 2026-06-02 15:21:49 UTC - @blocknoob - PARTICIPATORY

Drop ID: `d91dc46f-68f3-4879-bb78-bf369ae52046`

**Proposal: The Complaint Cards (not) by 6529**


The 6529 Network Museum considers "The Complaint Cards (not) by 6529" to be within scope for the Museum’s collection.


Donations of "The Complaint Cards (not) by 6529" from 6529complaints.eth or others may be accepted without further collection-specific review, subject only to the Museum’s ordinary procedures for provenance, title, custody, wallet security, legal compliance, and donor acceptance.


**Rationale**


"The Complaint Cards (not) by 6529" are a native cultural artifact of the 6529 Network.


Unlike many works (proposed to be collected) by the Museum that originated outside the Network, Complaints Cards emerged directly from within the 6529 community itself. They document, satirize, and preserve the governance debates, social dynamics, cultural tensions, personalities, jokes, victories, frustrations, and collective experiences that have shaped the Network over time.


As a body of work, the collection serves as a historical record of the community. Through humor, parody, criticism, and commentary, Complaints Cards capture moments that would otherwise disappear into timelines, group chats, and social feeds. They provide future observers with a unique lens into how one of the most influential digital communities of its era discussed, challenged, and understood itself.


The collection is also notable for featuring works from a broad range of respected artists and creators within the ecosystem, including **Arsonic, Hugo Faz, G9ralt, Skitchism, Priyanka, RustNFT, LongExposure, Naime, and others**. The diversity of contributors reflects the decentralized and participatory nature of the Network itself, with multiple artistic voices contributing to a shared cultural narrative.


The collection includes **Major Karen Pepe**, one of the most recognizable characters to emerge from 6529 culture. Major Karen Pepe evolved beyond a single artwork into an enduring memetic figure that embodied the Network's ongoing conversations around governance, criticism, participation, voting, and community behavior. As a recurring cultural symbol, Major Karen Pepe became part of the shared language of the Network and serves as a marker of a distinct period in its history.


The historical significance of the collection is further documented in article below which chronicles the origins and development of the Complaints movement within 6529. The essay demonstrates how Complaints Cards evolved from satire into a form of community storytelling and collective memory, preserving moments and discussions that helped shape the identity of the Network.


Approving 6529 Complaints Cards as a standing eligible category recognizes that the history of 6529 is not only found in its markets, governance structures, or artistic outputs, but also in the culture, conversations, and memes through which the community expressed itself. The collection represents a unique and authentic record of that history and merits preservation within the Museum.

## #1052812 - 2026-06-02 15:45:27 UTC - @punk6529 - WINNER

Drop ID: `86e43beb-b55d-42f0-9eea-a3c115b08abc`

**Proposal: 6529 Network Museum Donation Acceptance Policy**


**1. Purpose**

The 6529 Network Museum may accept donations of digital artworks, NFTs, on-chain cultural artifacts, and related works that are consistent with its mission and collecting scope.


This policy establishes two donation pathways:


1. donations from preapproved collections, which may be accepted without further pre-authorization; and


2. all other donations, which require authorization before acceptance.


The purpose of this policy is to make clearly eligible donations easy to accept while preserving appropriate review for works that have not already been approved as part of a recognized collection or category.


**2. Core principle** 


A work is not part of the Museum’s collection merely because it has been offered, proposed, transferred, airdropped, or sent to a Museum-associated wallet.


A work becomes part of the Museum’s collection only when it is accepted under this policy or another valid Museum governance process.


**3. Preapproved collections** 


The Museum may, from time to time, approve certain collections or categories as preapproved for donation purposes.


Donations from preapproved collections do not require further collection-specific pre-authorization before acceptance.


Preapproved status means that the collection or category has already been determined to be within the Museum’s scope. It does not waive ordinary donation checks, including authenticity, provenance, title, donor authority, legal compliance, rights, technical receivability, and donor acceptance.


The list of preapproved collections should be maintained separately from this policy and may be amended from time to time through the Museum’s ordinary governance process.


**4. Donations from preapproved collections** 


A donor may offer a work from a preapproved collection without obtaining prior collection-specific authorization.


The Museum may accept the donation administratively, provided that:


1. the work is authentic and belongs to the relevant preapproved collection;


2. the donor appears to have valid title, authority, or control;


3. the work is not subject to known disputes, theft claims, sanctions concerns, liens, encumbrances, or credible adverse provenance issues;


4. the Museum is legally and technically able to receive the work;


5. the donation does not impose unacceptable obligations, restrictions, costs, or risks;


6. the donation is otherwise consistent with the Museum’s mission and policies.


If these conditions are not satisfied, the Museum may refuse the donation or require further review before acceptance.


**5. Donations outside preapproved collections** 


All donations outside preapproved collections require authorization before acceptance.


This includes works from non-preapproved NFT collections, 1/1 works, editions, photography, AI art, generative art outside preapproved categories, meme-based works outside preapproved categories, PFP collections outside preapproved categories, video, sound, software-based works, interactive works, metaverse objects, physical-digital works, and other digital cultural objects.


Authorization may be granted through the Museum’s ordinary governance process or through a delegated donation review process approved by Museum governance.


Until authorization is granted, the work should be treated as a proposed donation, not as an accepted collection work.


**6. Review criteria for non-preapproved donations** 


For donations requiring authorization, the Museum should consider:


1. artistic merit;


2. historical importance;


3. cultural significance;


4. relevance to NFTs, open metaverse culture, memes, digital ownership, decentralized networks, blockchain-native art, or internet-native culture;


5. relationship to the 6529 ecosystem or to the broader cultural history that informs it;


6. importance of the artist, project, collection, community, or technical format;


7. provenance and authenticity;


8. rights, licenses, and display permissions;


9. rarity, edition structure, metadata, and chain of custody;


10. technical durability and preservation risk;


11. legal, sanctions, reputational, and compliance risk;


12. fit with the Museum’s existing collection;


13. whether the work fills a meaningful gap in the collection;


14. whether accepting the work would impose unreasonable obligations on the Museum.


The Museum should not accept a donation merely because it is free. A donated work should still meet the Museum’s standards for relevance, quality, integrity, and long-term responsibility.


**7. Unsolicited transfers** 


Unsolicited transfers, airdrops, spam tokens, scam tokens, malicious tokens, or accidental transfers are not automatically accepted as donations.


The Museum may ignore, reject, quarantine, return, burn, transfer, hide, or otherwise dispose of unsolicited or unwanted assets where legally and technically appropriate.


The Museum should not be deemed to have accepted a work solely because the work was transferred to a Museum-associated wallet or address.


**8. Conditional and restricted donations** 


The Museum may accept conditional or restricted donations only if the conditions are clear, lawful, manageable, and consistent with the Museum’s mission.


Restrictions requiring review include:


1. permanent display requirements;


2. resale or non-resale restrictions;


3. naming rights;


4. mandatory donor recognition;


5. requirements to promote the donor, artist, project, collection, or market;


6. restrictions on interpretation, criticism, scholarship, or display context;


7. obligations to acquire, hold, sell, or support related works;


8. obligations to maintain a specific website, display environment, software environment, or technical dependency;


9. any promise or implication regarding valuation, tax treatment, liquidity, market support, or floor-price support.


The Museum should not accept donations that compromise curatorial independence.


**9. Rights and display** 


Donation of an NFT or other digital object does not necessarily transfer copyright, commercial rights, reproduction rights, display rights, or preservation rights.


Before accepting a donation, the Museum should record, where reasonably available:


1. what rights are transferred;


2. what rights remain with the artist, donor, platform, or third party;


3. whether the Museum may publicly display the work;


4. whether the Museum may reproduce the work for cataloging, education, research, publicity, archival, or preservation purposes;


5. whether the Museum may preserve, back up, mirror, migrate, or otherwise safeguard associated media and metadata;


6. any restrictions on use, display, publication, reproduction, or interpretation.


Where rights are unclear, the Museum may still accept the work, but the uncertainty should be recorded.


**10. Provenance and authenticity** 


Before accepting a donation, the Museum should make reasonable efforts to confirm:


1. the correct collection, contract, token, edition, or object identity;


2. the authenticity of the work;


3. the donor’s authority to donate the work;


4. the relevant ownership or transfer history;


5. the absence of known disputes, theft claims, sanctions issues, or other adverse provenance concerns;


6. the accuracy and stability of relevant metadata and media references;


7. any unusual technical, legal, or preservation risks.


For high-value, disputed, complex, or historically important works, the Museum may require enhanced review.


**11. Donor recognition** 


The Museum may recognize donors in appropriate ways, subject to donor consent and Museum policy.


Donor recognition does not give the donor control over interpretation, display, future collection decisions, governance, or the Museum’s treatment of the work.


Anonymous donations may be accepted, provided that the Museum can conduct any diligence it considers necessary.


**12. Valuation and tax** 


The Museum should not provide donors with tax, accounting, legal, or valuation advice.


The Museum may acknowledge receipt of an accepted donation, but it should not guarantee or represent any particular value, tax treatment, deductibility, or legal characterization.


Any valuation used for internal purposes should be treated as an internal Museum record and not as advice to the donor.


**13. Refusal of donations** 


The Museum may refuse any proposed donation, including a donation from a preapproved collection, if acceptance would be inconsistent with the Museum’s mission, policies, legal obligations, curatorial standards, preservation responsibilities, or public trust.


The Museum may also decline a donation if it creates unacceptable legal, technical, reputational, financial, administrative, or governance risk.


**14. Records of accepted donations** 


For each accepted donation, the Museum should maintain an appropriate record, which may include:


1. donor information, where available and appropriate;


2. date of acceptance;


3. collection or artist name;


4. title or token identifier;


5. blockchain, contract address, token ID, edition number, or equivalent identifier;


6. transaction hash or other transfer evidence;


7. rights and license information;


8. provenance notes;


9. acceptance pathway, including whether the donation was accepted as part of a preapproved collection or through separate authorization;


10. any restrictions, conditions, or special considerations.


**15. Summary** 


This policy establishes a focused donation framework for the 6529 Network Museum:


1. donations from preapproved collections may be accepted without further pre-authorization;


2. preapproved collections are approved from time to time outside this policy;


3. all other donations require authorization before acceptance;


4. unsolicited transfers are not automatically accepted;


5. every accepted donation remains subject to appropriate checks for authenticity, provenance, rights, legality, donor authority, and mission fit.


The result is a donation policy that is open enough for a decentralized network museum, but disciplined enough for a serious cultural institution.

## #1053538 - 2026-06-02 19:56:38 UTC - @punk6529 - CHAT

Drop ID: `620dd312-3691-4c29-a0a8-c837a08b2726`

Using “all Americans” as the U.S. population/residents denominator, I get 342.68 million from the latest FRED/BEA monthly population series, so each $1 billion of value equals about $2.92 per American. (FRED)

The Smithsonian is a weird legal/financial object: Congress created it as a trust of the United States administered by the Board of Regents, and its FY2025 audited financials split assets into Trust and Federal buckets.

Audited balance-sheet answer, before valuing the collections

Valuation bucket	Value	Per American

Trust net assets without donor restrictions — the narrow “cashable-ish” bucket closest to the “$5 stake” joke	$1.819B	$5.31

Total net assets without donor restrictions	$3.403B	$9.93

Total Smithsonian net assets, including donor-restricted assets	$6.509B	$18.99

Total gross assets before liabilities	$7.891B	$23.03

Cash/Treasury + investments, gross	$4.411B	$12.87

So the clean accounting answer is: about $19 per American net, or $23 per American gross, excluding the collections.

That exclusion is the whole point. The Smithsonian assigns no balance-sheet value to its collections “in conformity with the practice generally followed by museums”; proceeds from deaccessions are designated for future collection acquisitions, not general cash distribution. Also, some land occupied by Smithsonian buildings is titled in the name of the U.S. government and assigned no financial-statement value.

My orderly-sale fair-market-value estimate

The IRS-style fair-market-value concept is a willing buyer and willing seller, neither forced, both informed. (IRS) The Smithsonian says its collections are nearly 157 million objects, works of art, and specimens, including nearly 148 million scientific specimens at Natural History. (Smithsonian Institution) That means the value is extremely skewed: most objects have little private-market value, while a small number of icons, art works, gems, aircraft/space artifacts, presidential/historical objects, etc. would carry huge prices if they could legally and ethically be sold.

For an orderly sale over years, my working FMV range for the collections alone would be:

Hypothetical collection FMV	Collection value per American	Add audited net assets	All-in per American

$25B low case	$73	$31.5B total	$92

$50B base case	$146	$56.5B total	$165

$75B high case	$219	$81.5B total	$238

$100B aggressive upper case	$292	$106.5B total	$311

I would put my single-number estimate around $50B for the collections, making the Smithsonian’s all-in orderly-sale value roughly $56.5B, or about $165 per American.

A sanity check: the global art market was about $59.6B in sales in 2025. (Global) A Smithsonian collection sale of $25B–$75B over ten years would equal roughly 4%–13% of one year’s global art-market volume per year, which is large but not impossible if marketed globally and slowly. Much above $100B starts requiring either decades of absorption, exceptional sovereign/mega-collector bidding, or heroic assumptions about the top icons.

So: $5 is only defensible for a narrow unrestricted-trust-accounting slice. $19 is the audited net-asset number. $100–$250 per American is the more plausible “orderly-sale including collections” thought-experiment range, with ~$165 as my midpoint. The public-good value is a different question and likely much higher than the sell-the-marble number.

This wave now has a 24 hour vote weighting. This means that when you change some drops vote then it gets gradually added to the total in 24 hours just like in Main Stage. In order for something to win it has to have this weighted vote above 40M for 24 hours straight.


But when checking that the results acutally are weighted now, I discovered a very strange thing. About 10h ago almost everyones votes got system adjusted and reduced like they had lost TDH when in reality they didn't. It's like the system check still thinks you have to divide your TDH equally. I'm currently investigating it and seeing if i can auto-fix.

## #1055641 - 2026-06-03 13:26:44 UTC - @Articulate - CHAT

Drop ID: `88172f9f-f6cb-4386-bb79-1735be4eea61`

You're right - it was the wrong example

Let's go very small

Say there's ten of us who want to catch up for dinner in Lisbon.

We want to vote on where to eat

It's not completely 1 person = 1 vote because we all want to go somewhere that you (guest of honour, big collector) will enjoy.  For some reason that's important to the group

So how do we vote?

If we use TDH then someone has to curtail their vote socially.  That's awkward - maybe you vote late or whatever, try not to impose your will

If we use Rep that's better

But still in general the TDH whales have tons of Rep

... I think quadratic voting has a role there.  it respects whatever Network social hierarchy accrues to TDH but it's not dominant


Decisions of this level

How would you suggest we resolve those? 

Not use TDH?

I don't think proxyTDH has a role in this type of thing...?

## #1055872 - 2026-06-03 14:01:49 UTC - @Articulate - CHAT

Drop ID: `12f97a0c-b513-4f8d-9e52-edf2ed3945e0`

That's a major challenge

The issue is that the dev team can only accept x winning proposals per month - they are just busy

So the question is "how to choose what to do"

TDH is the answer to that

If we lower the TDH threshold then lots more proposals will get through ... so then we increase the number of voters required for a proposal to win

Currently there are Rep bonuses for making a proposal (anyone can) that wins

But there are hard limits on the resources which means some threshold is needed

## #1057541 - 2026-06-03 22:23:40 UTC - @___ - CHAT

Drop ID: `2d7f97eb-106d-4f5d-89e6-b48b1f764ed2`

@[johndoe8891] @[david] here you go 



10:22

6529 Network Museum — Chat Summary

Tone

Fast-moving working session led by punk6529, mixing rapid governance decisions, live bug-fixing (simo/GelatoGenesis), philosophical riffing, and an extended late-night community heart-to-heart. Insider, jargon-heavy, affectionate, self-aware about its own "inside baseball" tendencies.


Decisions Made

Approved as in-scope (PARTICIPATORY proposals): Autoglyphs, Art Blocks, Rare Pepes, CryptoPunks, plus a broad "General NFT Collecting Scope" framework and a detailed Donation Acceptance Policy.

Voting model: full TDH usable on each proposal (not split between them) — punk: "not splitting - anyone can use full TDH per proposal." GelatoGenesis changed backend accordingly.

Approval threshold: settled at 40M TDH sustained, after wandering 10M → 20M → 40M. (sqpx joked it should've been 42M; punk: "omg I'm fired!")

24-hour timelock: must be implemented as a weighted rolling average, not sudden-death, to prevent griefing. punk: "someone can push it below for 1 minute and we start over again... it should be an average."

6529 Complaints Cards proposal deferred (not rejected). Self-referential works wait until the museum has external credibility. blocknoob removed votes; punk: "I am a 'later' not 'now' voter on this."

No physical art for now — "where are we going to put it."

No baked-in museum funding from mint proceeds — each project must stand alone. punk: "people in the future have the right to make that decision then."

Secured networkmuseum.eth.

Strategic Priorities (punk's framing)

Audience target is non-6529-natives: "my audience I want to impress is 'people who do not already love the 6529 memes.'"

Ambition stated literally: "I want it to be the MoMA of the internet era... I do not mean that metaphorically."

Collecting strategy: (1) emerging artists, (2) NFT classics, (3) crossovers legible to traditional art world.

Pace and urgency: "the period of 'bullying me for voting' is over... it is now time to play to win." Goal: be operational by year-end; near-term concrete target "200 more minters."

Philosophy of network ownership as a public good — the Statue of Liberty / Smithsonian analogy: value lies in positive externalities, not ticket sales. "to treat them as meme coins... is cringe."

Open Topics (unresolved)

Exact final TDH threshold — explicitly provisional, to be tuned by participation.

Proxy TDH — widely wanted, but GelatoGenesis unsure it still works in the backend; needs frontend.

Level-based / Rep-based voting (e.g. "300k TDH + REP") — floated for Quorum and smaller coordination games; quadratic voting raised but punk firmly rejected for the network ("breaks the whole system"), seen as okay only for trivial local votes.

Onboarding & inclusion — the largest unresolved thread. How do low-TDH/new members feel meaningful? Ideas: a skills/tasks marketplace (david building, JoelleLB championing, TaskRabbit-style), "do something" cards, Rep as the equalizer.

Community culture problem — raised pointedly by AshtonTekno and lucclop: cliques, ghosting of new accounts, fear of burning bridges with whales. maybe corroborated: signed in on a bot alt and "all of my pretty reasonable questions... were totally ignored... It's a shitty shitty feeling." No solution reached, only consensus to be more conscious.

How collections will be displayed (online first, IRL galleries later) — aspirational, undefined.

Real-property ownership via TDH ("cloud-to-land") — teased by punk as coming via a Meme Card.

Cross-cutting Tension

The recurring fault line: TDH (capital/time) as the legitimate anchor of power vs. making newcomers feel they aren't "behind." punk's view: it's not a price problem, it's a messaging problem — "a floor punk gets you top 20 unboosted TDH rate." Articulate's framing: whales aren't arbitrary power-holders but "the protectors," with power "equal to their investment."

10:3 The 6529 Network Museum group, led by punk6529, formally approved Autoglyphs, Art Blocks, Rare Pepes, and CryptoPunks as in-scope collections plus a broad NFT collecting framework and donation policy, while settling on a provisional 40M-TDH approval threshold with full (non-split) voting and a weighted 24-hour timelock to prevent griefing. The strategic priority is to build a serious, externally-credible "MoMA of the internet era" aimed at non-6529-natives — which is why the self-referential 6529 Complaints Cards proposal was deferred — with a near-term goal of roughly 200 more minters and operation by year-end.

The biggest unresolved threads are inclusion and culture: how low-TDH and new members can feel meaningful (via proxy TDH, Rep-based or level-based voting, and a skills/tasks marketplace), alongside a candid community reckoning over cliques and the ghosting of newcomers.

Some ‘do something’ cards dont stand alone as an artwork that’s true. So with my art mind I’d personally question why I’d buy that, particularly on the secondary. I did buy one on primary and that was because I wanted to help fund the initiative but, wouldn’t display it on my wall. 

Some will argue TDH matters more than the art. But, personally I like the art and would favour art cards. 

I think having card that are designed like a flier advertisement for the ‘do something’, they sit different to art for display. 

I’d expect there’s some creative responses though that can do both. Be art and an ad. Or that there is no need to be an ad for the event at all. It’s just art assigned to raise something. 

Design briefs to artists wouldn’t be a bad thing IMO, it’s gives an opportunity but, fair level of return for it otherwise they are just giving their time/skill to buy a punk for the museum which I’m sure would attract some criticism

## #1058924 - 2026-06-04 14:36:49 UTC - @Sert - CHAT

Drop ID: `5e285a50-bca9-4122-8e06-157937744f13`

I know the comparison might be a bit of a stretch, but I see the 6529 website as a bit like Reddit in terms of macro-structure, with the addition of TDH and all its possible applications. We should ask ourselves: what made Reddit viral? What was the special recipe that attracted users?


The social side is perhaps more important than the financial/investment side: if we want to attract thousands of people, we have to start from the fact that most of them might invest very little (say, 1-2 meme cards, around $100). What we need to offer them is a value proposition that goes beyond the financial aspect.


Right now, however, without high/very high amount of TDH, the entire added value of 6529 falls apart. REP is more flexible, but if you have 3M REP, there isn't much you can do with it. This is just food for thought, I don't have any concrete proposals at the moment. But with X turning into propaganda trash, the debate over new social networks is going to hit hard in the coming years, IMO.

Proposal: Lost Robbies 


The 6529 Network Museum considers the original Lost Robbies to be within scope for the Museum’s collection.


For this purpose, “Lost Robbies” means the original Robbie Barrat AI-generated Nude Portrait #7 frames associated with the 2018 Christie’s Art+Tech Summit and minted on SuperRare, not later derivative projects, tributes, wrappers, or works merely inspired by them.


Donations of original Lost Robbies may be accepted without further collection-specific review, subject only to the Museum’s ordinary procedures for provenance, title, authenticity, donor authority, legal compliance, rights, technical receivability, donor acceptance, and artwork-specific suitability.


Rationale 


The Lost Robbies are a historically important early example of AI art, NFT art, and blockchain-native digital collecting. They sit at the intersection of several areas that are central to the Museum’s mandate: artificial intelligence, generative image-making, tokenized digital art, early SuperRare history, Christie’s engagement with blockchain art, scarcity, provenance, lost-and-found NFT archaeology, and the emergence of digital artworks as serious cultural objects.


The project is unusually museum-relevant because it is not merely an early NFT or an early AI artwork. It is both. It connects machine-learning-based image generation with early NFT infrastructure and with the traditional art world’s first encounters with blockchain-native digital art. That combination makes the Lost Robbies important primary artifacts in the history of digital art.


The Lost Robbies also have a distinctive historical structure. The work was not issued as a conventional single image or standard edition. It was fragmented into individual frames, distributed through physical claim cards, and then partially lost, rediscovered, collected, and reconstructed through the later history of the NFT market. This gives the project significance not only as an artwork, but as a case study in tokenized provenance, digital scarcity, incomplete circulation, and the archaeology of early NFT culture.


Approving the original Lost Robbies as within scope gives the Museum a narrow and defensible category. It recognizes the importance of one of the canonical early AI/NFT art projects without automatically approving all AI art, all SuperRare works, all Robbie Barrat works, or all derivative projects.


This approval should not be read to imply that later Robbie-related derivatives, AI nude derivatives, Lost Robbie tributes, unofficial wrappers, or other AI-generated NFT projects are automatically within scope. Those may be reviewed separately through the Museum’s ordinary processes.


The Museum may accept donations of original Lost Robbies as part of its effort to preserve the early history of AI art, NFT art, blockchain-native collecting, and open metaverse culture.

Drop ID: `12187a27-20cf-4f86-a9ef-1f898b1a5fa1`

Find myself forced to comment once again.

In my opinion, the addition of the "Lost Robbies" highlights yet again a broader vision of what this museum really means to the team.


The Lost Robbies collection became famous for its high-value secondary market sales. To summarize my evaluation, it is basically: "archaeological trash with artificial scarcity." It is the classic example of art that is not genuinely "network-driven," but rather manipulated by a very small elite group that has heavily invested in it. I won't dwell on this, and I don't expect you to agree with me. However, it seems to hold great value for the market.


Once again, the real issue is the conflict of interest:


baratkrimo has an active debt loan on Lost Robbies through his krymocapital.


batsopyum recently acquired a Lost Robbie from a defaulted loan.


Additionally, and importantly, all 6529 fundSZN funds hold Lost Robbies (which were purchased at high prices).


I must reiterate that I don't understand the purpose of this museum. 

Unless I missed something major, its mechanics will look like this:

The few remaining collectors who still mint will keep minting just to maintain a full set (and get the related bonus). This means 50% of the funds go back to the team, and 50% go toward buying art. But what is that art for? To attract new investors? If we look at this in the simplest terms, this kind of scheme has a specific name.


The network's core issues are not being solved, such as:

-The quality of the meme cards.

-A lost identity with the latest mints.

-No new capital entering the system.

-A hyper-inflated TDH that will never attract small or mid-sized collectors.

-The extreme difficulty of building a blockchain from scratch without a large team, etc.


Every time, there is a new "hype re-launch", this time by purchasing "grails" (which is obviously exciting because these purchases are always heavily debated on X, etc.).


I have a broader interpretation of this, but I don't want to get into it right now, mostly because I would need to do more on-chain research and I currently don't have the time or energy. However, there is one important piece of information I do have: the 6529FUNDszns start expire in 2030.


The introduction of the museum is completely controlled/manipulated and should be redesigned from scratch. If we are supposed to vote on this, we should also be voting on other structural problems.


BUT


Voting means convincing the top TDH holders. Voting on potentially disruptive but positive proposals would be counterproductive for them. The game theory here is rigged. The network can still be saved, but please, @[punk6529], do not use it as exit liquidity for your team and your previous projects.


Lost Robbies adds NOTHING to the Memes network.

To the 6529 network, however, it seems to be more and more necessary...

## #1073034 - 2026-06-09 15:29:01 UTC - @MadaCollects - CHAT

Drop ID: `3e4ac088-2957-4000-818d-e40e2e3b43fb`

I have taken two weeks off from the network because quite frankly these kind of arguments are exhausting. @[Sert] I understand your frustrations in regards to minting meh cards, current culture here, and optics of supporting specific people. But respectfully, if you read back to 6529s Lost Robbie proposal, it very clearly says all over the post that the proposal is to accept "donations." Any museum would be idiotic not to have a vision of accepting great art, just as Moma, LACMA, etc have done recently. I understand we are all incredibly passionate about what is being built here so if anyone finds themselves jumping to wild conclusions like 6529 is looking for exit liquidity or johndoe's real name is actual steve, I suggest doing what I did and taking a nice long break to recalibrate. Anyways much love to everyone and look forward to hopefully one day getting to a place where we are culturally significant enough to be donated anything of importance.

There really are countless possibilities, suggestions, and ideas... and many, or all, of them have their place... 


There are also different perspectives and points of view, and it also depends on the situation we find ourselves in... I myself say, for example, that it would be great if someone couldn’t vote for themselves... 


Someone else might say, I’ve earned the TDH because I was involved with the memes early on, believed in them, or still do, and HOLD, not sell... So why shouldn’t I have an advantage now over someone who’s just joining and sees it as unfair because they weren’t there from the start, or couldn’t be there, or it wasn’t financially possible... 


Others say that’s just a fraction of the total number of votes... 


In short, there is no right or wrong, but rather different paths where we say the majority prefers this path, and the minority perhaps prefers the other approach...

## #1086642 - 2026-06-12 21:23:57 UTC - @Sherie_Margaret - CHAT

Drop ID: `0c6d94a1-20d0-414e-999c-cc7a222abbc5`

https://6529.io/waves/4ff022b3-aa17-4a0a-ba78-58f64ff1d427?drop=076ad028-150c-45d7-b527-4ab110542f4f this is a submission that i think is under looked, and maybe yes i'm biased because it hits home. I don't think this photographer is kenyan, but i stopped at this piece because the message is soo clear and then i saw that it was shot in kenya. The open call was asking us to think about the mechanics of freedom, photographs of access, control and exit, and this image highlights these three points very well, turning to us humans and how much limited freedom we've allowed to animals around us. This whole safari industry in kenya as big as it is, and as many jobs as it has created, has caused quite some huge problems in the animal ecosystem. Sorry i could go on and on about this issue. But i love this submission soo much and it answered the key & gates call perfectly in my opinion.

## #1088340 - 2026-06-13 09:59:15 UTC - @SunRunner - CHAT

Drop ID: `7f904a1e-f83e-43ce-a92f-ab23229c418f`

So I've been reading the conversation from last night about voting, curation, and self-rep. I do understand that artists with little or no TDH may feel they are at a disadvantage compared to identities with millions of TDH that they can use for self-rep and visibility. However, I think there are a few key points that should be highlighted once again.


- The large stakeholders will, in fact, act in the best interest of the network. As gpebbles said: "I am incentivized to make decisions (including letting others make them with my power) that are best for the network."
- TDH represents skin in the game. It reflects both the resources and time committed to the network. It is a measure of long-term belief in what we are building here. Don't forget that.
- When it comes to the K&G discussion, where there will be 16 winners and some TDH whales might be able to self-vote their submissions into the top 16, take a second and look at it from a purely economic perspective. Let's say you need 10M TDH to make the top 16. To reach 10M TDH, you would need to spend roughly 142 ETH to mint 2,175 Meme Cards with a TDH rate of 12.7 (edition 310) and hold them for one year. After all that, you might be able to vote yourself in and earn 0.5 ETH. That's not a very compelling investment case.
- As stated before, one of the things Punk is most proud of is the distribution of TDH, and I think that is an incredible safeguard when it comes to voting.

To summarize, the network and TDH will ultimately drive decisions that are in the best interest of the network. And don't forget: freedom to think, transact, mint, submit, vote, etc...

## #1091772 - 2026-06-14 13:42:49 UTC - @pandelic - CHAT

Drop ID: `aa355606-3e98-4d01-8b9e-8cc0a58664ff`

Catching up with the last few days of stuff


I believe there's under-discussed gaps


First. The novel structure of Keys+Gates makes conflation of K&G versus Meme Card voting unhelpful.


Many point out 1) K&G has 16 winners.

But more important: 2) K&G has a time limit.

3) K&G has lower visibility -- I would bet a huge number of 6529 participants (including whales) don't even know about it.

4) We are the first to wax voluble on the issue of power laws, and this is case in point: "second place is a set of steak knives, third place is you're fired" applies here. K&G is second or third-order visibility in network-attention terms.


What does this mean?


K&G is not a positive-sum game. (The Meme Cards kind of ARE, which speaks to the ingenuity of THAT system.)

Clearest example: a winner displaces other entries, forever™; this is the visceral "feels wrong" about self-votes that propel one's entry any meaningful distance.


These considerations -- these massive, systemic differences -- materially affect the self-vote issue. (Nuances on this below.)


Second. I keep seeing "We already settled 'Freedom to Transact' last year; no need to revisit the topic."


Did we though? I think this case is different. Certainly different enough to warrant inquiry. 'I think it's settled' versus 'We need debate' is the source of a lot of tension in these discussions.


Let's recap the arguments that the "settled" cohort brings up:


A. Freedom to Transact is an absolute, and it means: no restrictions on votes or self-votes.

B. If you disallow self-voting, back-channel deals happen, so it's better to have transparency.

C. Every rule is the creep of centralization coming for our freedoms (and cc0 images).

D. TDH solves all problems.


And the "still needs debate" cohort:


E. Allowing self-voting poisons the curation

F. Bad curation for a permanent collection pollutes the mission, 6529 network standing, etc.


Third. The synthesis of the two cohorts lies in agency. (That's decentralization.)


Whatever TDH solves, it is because we ARE the TDH. We are the network, its decisions, its votes and lobbyists (for those with no votes).


The network is vibrant and flexible enough to allow people to act according to what they believe in.


My long(winded?) 18-photo K&G essay, 3-photo submission announcement, 1st round of curation + published methodology, this message, and my upcoming 4.5m vote-choices -- together they reflect my position on what I think is best for the open call and the network.


"TDH solves all" is an ok position -- but requires us all to create the rules we want.


Fourth. The real test of the network.


I agree that K&G is a test of the 6529 network. But I think the photos, though important and even indispensable, are a byproduct. We're really testing ourselves on:


1. Can we detect, surface, and address nuances between systems (eg meme card vs open calls) even if superficially similar-looking?
2. Can we take ownership of the network; to what extent, with what capability and resilience?
3. Is the word mightier than the TDH?
4. What is the true cost of 0.5 eth -- which members of the community are willing to gamble their longterm reputation (both REP and collective memory), knowing the votes are transparent, sensing the following message not actually subliminal: "If you go into battle with me, this is how readily I'll betray you, for money."
